Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those times when you had a lot of energy, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t have called it “euphoric” at the time, maybe just very optimistic. Almost brilliantly optimistic, really, like every obstacle was suddenly small and I could see the sensible way through everything.\n\nThough… I should say, if someone questioned me, I didn’t take it very well. I’d snap at them, as if they were trying to interfere or sabotage things. I’m not proud of that. At the time it felt like I was the only one seeing clearly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long do these periods of feeling optimistic and having a lot of energy typically last?\n\nPatient: It went on longer than I’d like to admit, really. After that investment loss, I think I was… not quite living like my usual retired self for about five months. Most of that time, everything seemed to revolve around trying to undo the damage.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of duration you’re asking about, doctor, but it wasn’t just a few days.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone would, especially when things weren’t going well. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear period of being sad in the way you mean.\n\nIs there something particular I should be looking for when you say depressed?\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel irritable during those times when you were lying awake before dawn and not feeling rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable is probably fair. I don’t like admitting that, because I thought of myself as focused, not angry. But if someone questioned what I was doing, I could snap at them pretty quickly.\n\nAt the time it felt like I was being sensible and optimistic, and everyone else was getting in the way. Looking back, I suppose I was acting as if I were the only reasonable person in the room. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that weren't really there during those periods of high energy or irritability?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was wound up and probably interpreting things too confidently, but I don’t recall hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there.\n\nIf there’s a more subtle version of that you mean, I can try to think about it, but nothing like voices or visions comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a persistent and profound sadness that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has been more than two weeks. I’ve tried not to call it “profound,” because that sounds rather dramatic, but since those plans fell apart, I’ve been low and… withdrawn, and feeling guilty.\n\nIt’s been going on about nine months now. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but I think that’s the accurate answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but the things I used to genuinely enjoy just feel… flat now.

Almost brilliantly optimistic, really, like every obstacle was suddenly small and I could see the sensible way through everything.\n\nThough… I should say, if someone questioned me, I didn’t take it very well. I’d snap at them, as if they were trying to interfere or sabotage things. I’m not proud of that. At the time it felt like I was the only one seeing clearly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long do these periods of feeling optimistic and having a lot of energy typically last?\n\nPatient: It went on longer than I’d like to admit, really. After that investment loss, I think I was… not quite living like my usual retired self for about five months. Most of that time, everything seemed to revolve around trying to undo the damage.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of duration you’re asking about, doctor, but it wasn’t just a few days.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone would, especially when things weren’t going well. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear period of being sad in the way you mean.\n\nIs there something particular I should be looking for when you say depressed?\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel irritable during those times when you were lying awake before dawn and not feeling rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable is probably fair. I don’t like admitting that, because I thought of myself as focused, not angry. But if someone questioned what I was doing, I could snap at them pretty quickly.\n\nAt the time it felt like I was being sensible and optimistic, and everyone else was getting in the way. Looking back, I suppose I was acting as if I were the only reasonable person in the room. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that weren't really there during those periods of high energy or irritability?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was wound up and probably interpreting things too confidently, but I don’t recall hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there.\n\nIf there’s a more subtle version of that you mean, I can try to think about it, but nothing like voices or visions comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a persistent and profound sadness that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has been more than two weeks. I’ve tried not to call it “profound,” because that sounds rather dramatic, but since those plans fell apart, I’ve been low and… withdrawn, and feeling guilty.\n\nIt’s been going on about nine months now. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but I think that’s the accurate answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy during this period of feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but the things I used to genuinely enjoy just feel… flat now.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or get completely absorbed in photography and cooking. Even bridge, sometimes. Lately I’ll look at my camera or think about going to the bridge table, and I don’t feel pleasure so much as the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying it.\n\nI suppose that counts as losing interest, doesn’t it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

At first I’d sleep two or three hours and then be up, making plans and feeling like I was ready to take on the day. I didn’t think it was such a terrible thing then.\n\nBut now it’s not feeling so useful. I’m lying awake before dawn with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. I thought I should come in before I let it go on too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing periods where you have a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I didn’t think of it as a problem at first, because it felt like I was being productive. I know that may sound a bit excessive, but at the time it felt… purposeful. I wouldn’t have called it “euphoric” at the time, maybe just very optimistic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s mostly my sleep, doctor. It’s gotten… a bit out of hand, I think. At first I’d sleep two or three hours and then be up, making plans and feeling like I was ready to take on the day. I didn’t think it was such a terrible thing then.\n\nBut now it’s not feeling so useful. I’m lying awake before dawn with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. I thought I should come in before I let it go on too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing periods where you have a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I didn’t think of it as a problem at first, because it felt like I was being productive. But it was like I had this engine running inside me, if that makes sense, and sitting quietly felt almost impossible.\n\nI’d be up before sunrise, making calls, rewriting proposals, going from one thing to the next without really stopping. I know that may sound a bit excessive, but at the time it felt… purposeful. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those times when you had a lot of energy, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t have called it “euphoric” at the time, maybe just very optimistic. Almost brilliantly optimistic, really, like every obstacle was suddenly small and I could see the sensible way through everything.\n\nThough… I should say, if someone questioned me, I didn’t take it very well. I’d snap at them, as if they were trying to interfere or sabotage things. I’m not proud of that. At the time it felt like I was the only one seeing clearly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long do these periods of feeling optimistic and having a lot of energy typically last?\n\nPatient: It went on longer than I’d like to admit, really. After that investment loss, I think I was… not quite living like my usual retired self for about five months. Most of that time, everything seemed to revolve around trying to undo the damage.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of duration you’re asking about, doctor, but it wasn’t just a few days.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone would, especially when things weren’t going well. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear period of being sad in the way you mean.\n\nIs there something particular I should be looking for when you say depressed?\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel irritable during those times when you were lying awake before dawn and not feeling rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable is probably fair. I don’t like admitting that, because I thought of myself as focused, not angry. But if someone questioned what I was doing, I could snap at them pretty quickly.\n\nAt the time it felt like I was being sensible and optimistic, and everyone else was getting in the way. Looking back, I suppose I was acting as if I were the only reasonable person in the room. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that weren't really there during those periods of high energy or irritability?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was wound up and probably interpreting things too confidently, but I don’t recall hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there.\n\nIf there’s a more subtle version of that you mean, I can try to think about it, but nothing like voices or visions comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a persistent and profound sadness that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has been more than two weeks. I’ve tried not to call it “profound,” because that sounds rather dramatic, but since those plans fell apart, I’ve been low and… withdrawn, and feeling guilty.\n\nIt’s been going on about nine months now. At first I’d sleep two or three hours and then be up, making plans and feeling like I was ready to take on the day. I didn’t think it was such a terrible thing then.\n\nBut now it’s not feeling so useful. I’m lying awake before dawn with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. I thought I should come in before I let it go on too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing periods where you have a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I didn’t think of it as a problem at first, because it felt like I was being productive. But it was like I had this engine running inside me, if that makes sense, and sitting quietly felt almost impossible.\n\nI’d be up before sunrise, making calls, rewriting proposals, going from one thing to the next without really stopping. I know that may sound a bit excessive, but at the time it felt… purposeful. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those times when you had a lot of energy, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t have called it “euphoric” at the time, maybe just very optimistic. Almost brilliantly optimistic, really, like every obstacle was suddenly small and I could see the sensible way through everything.\n\nThough… I should say, if someone questioned me, I didn’t take it very well. I’d snap at them, as if they were trying to interfere or sabotage things. I’m not proud of that. At the time it felt like I was the only one seeing clearly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long do these periods of feeling optimistic and having a lot of energy typically last?\n\nPatient: It went on longer than I’d like to admit, really. After that investment loss, I think I was… not quite living like my usual retired self for about five months. Most of that time, everything seemed to revolve around trying to undo the damage.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of duration you’re asking about, doctor, but it wasn’t just a few days.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone would, especially when things weren’t going well. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear period of being sad in the way you mean.\n\nIs there something particular I should be looking for when you say depressed?\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel irritable during those times when you were lying awake before dawn and not feeling rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable is probably fair. I don’t like admitting that, because I thought of myself as focused, not angry. But if someone questioned what I was doing, I could snap at them pretty quickly.\n\nAt the time it felt like I was being sensible and optimistic, and everyone else was getting in the way. Looking back, I suppose I was acting as if I were the only reasonable person in the room. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that weren't really there during those periods of high energy or irritability?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was wound up and probably interpreting things too confidently, but I don’t recall hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there.\n\nIf there’s a more subtle version of that you mean, I can try to think about it, but nothing like voices or visions comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a persistent and profound sadness that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has been more than two weeks. I’ve tried not to call it “profound,” because that sounds rather dramatic, but since those plans fell apart, I’ve been low and… withdrawn, and feeling guilty.\n\nIt’s been going on about nine months now. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but I think that’s the accurate answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy during this period of feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but the things I used to genuinely enjoy just feel… flat now.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or get completely absorbed in photography and cooking. Even bridge, sometimes. Mostly I just think about how much effort it would take to act as if I were enjoying them.\n\nIs that what you mean by almost all activities?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I suppose I have, though I don’t like admitting that.\n\nIt’s not that I’ve made some grand plan, but there are mornings when I think it would be a relief if I simply didn’t wake up. And that scared me enough that I told my wife I shouldn’t be left alone.\n\nIs that the sort of thing you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your movement or activity level, such as feeling restless or slowed down?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychomotor_Changes\nmanifestation: \"My wife says I either pace through the house shuffling papers like I am late for a board meeting, or I sit so still at the kitchen table that it takes me a long time to answer a simple question.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… there has been some of that, I think.

My wife has noticed it more than I have. She says I’ll pace through the house, sort of shuffling papers around as if I’m late for some board meeting, though of course I’m not. And then at other times I’ll sit at the kitchen table so still that she has to wait quite a while for me to answer even a simple question.

I don’t know if that’s the kind of change you mean, but yes, I suppose it’s been noticeable.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s mostly my sleep, doctor. It’s gotten… a bit out of hand, I think. At first I’d sleep two or three hours and then be up, making plans and feeling like I was ready to take on the day. I didn’t think it was such a terrible thing then.\n\nBut now it’s not feeling so useful. I’m lying awake before dawn with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. I thought I should come in before I let it go on too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing periods where you have a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I didn’t think of it as a problem at first, because it felt like I was being productive. But it was like I had this engine running inside me, if that makes sense, and sitting quietly felt almost impossible.\n\nI’d be up before sunrise, making calls, rewriting proposals, going from one thing to the next without really stopping. I know that may sound a bit excessive, but at the time it felt… purposeful. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those times when you had a lot of energy, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t have called it “euphoric” at the time, maybe just very optimistic. Almost brilliantly optimistic, really, like every obstacle was suddenly small and I could see the sensible way through everything.\n\nThough… I should say, if someone questioned me, I didn’t take it very well. I’d snap at them, as if they were trying to interfere or sabotage things. I’m not proud of that. At the time it felt like I was the only one seeing clearly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long do these periods of feeling optimistic and having a lot of energy typically last?\n\nPatient: It went on longer than I’d like to admit, really. After that investment loss, I think I was… not quite living like my usual retired self for about five months. Most of that time, everything seemed to revolve around trying to undo the damage.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of duration you’re asking about, doctor, but it wasn’t just a few days.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone would, especially when things weren’t going well. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear period of being sad in the way you mean.\n\nIs there something particular I should be looking for when you say depressed?\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel irritable during those times when you were lying awake before dawn and not feeling rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable is probably fair. I don’t like admitting that, because I thought of myself as focused, not angry. But if someone questioned what I was doing, I could snap at them pretty quickly.\n\nAt the time it felt like I was being sensible and optimistic, and everyone else was getting in the way. Looking back, I suppose I was acting as if I were the only reasonable person in the room. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that weren't really there during those periods of high energy or irritability?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was wound up and probably interpreting things too confidently, but I don’t recall hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there.\n\nIf there’s a more subtle version of that you mean, I can try to think about it, but nothing like voices or visions comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a persistent and profound sadness that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has been more than two weeks. I’ve tried not to call it “profound,” because that sounds rather dramatic, but since those plans fell apart, I’ve been low and… withdrawn, and feeling guilty.\n\nIt’s been going on about nine months now. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but I think that’s the accurate answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy during this period of feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but the things I used to genuinely enjoy just feel… flat now.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or get completely absorbed in photography and cooking. Even bridge, sometimes. Lately I’ll look at my camera or think about going to the bridge table, and I don’t feel pleasure so much as the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying it.\n\nI suppose that counts as losing interest, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer take pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it just that they feel like too much effort?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s just effort, because that sounds more… manageable, I suppose. But no, it’s more than that.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or lose myself in photography and cooking. Lately I look at my camera, or even think about the bridge table, and I feel almost nothing—except the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying myself.\n\nIs that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed. I could check properly if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I have.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but I keep going back over that investment decision in my head. I thought I should come in before I let it go on too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing periods where you have a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I didn’t think of it as a problem at first, because it felt like I was being productive. I know that may sound a bit excessive, but at the time it felt… purposeful. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those times when you had a lot of energy, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t have called it “euphoric” at the time, maybe just very optimistic. I’d snap at them, as if they were trying to interfere or sabotage things. I’m not proud of that. At the time it felt like I was the only one seeing clearly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long do these periods of feeling optimistic and having a lot of energy typically last?\n\nPatient: It went on longer than I’d like to admit, really. After that investment loss, I think I was… not quite living like my usual retired self for about five months. Most of that time, everything seemed to revolve around trying to undo the damage.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of duration you’re asking about, doctor, but it wasn’t just a few days.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone would, especially when things weren’t going well. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear period of being sad in the way you mean.\n\nIs there something particular I should be looking for when you say depressed?\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel irritable during those times when you were lying awake before dawn and not feeling rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable is probably fair. I don’t like admitting that, because I thought of myself as focused, not angry. But if someone questioned what I was doing, I could snap at them pretty quickly.\n\nAt the time it felt like I was being sensible and optimistic, and everyone else was getting in the way. Looking back, I suppose I was acting as if I were the only reasonable person in the room. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that weren't really there during those periods of high energy or irritability?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was wound up and probably interpreting things too confidently, but I don’t recall hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there.\n\nIf there’s a more subtle version of that you mean, I can try to think about it, but nothing like voices or visions comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a persistent and profound sadness that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has been more than two weeks. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s mostly my sleep, doctor. It’s gotten… a bit out of hand, I think. At first I’d sleep two or three hours and then be up, making plans and feeling like I was ready to take on the day. I didn’t think it was such a terrible thing then.\n\nBut now it’s not feeling so useful. I’m lying awake before dawn with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. I thought I should come in before I let it go on too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing periods where you have a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I didn’t think of it as a problem at first, because it felt like I was being productive. But it was like I had this engine running inside me, if that makes sense, and sitting quietly felt almost impossible.\n\nI’d be up before sunrise, making calls, rewriting proposals, going from one thing to the next without really stopping. I know that may sound a bit excessive, but at the time it felt… purposeful. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those times when you had a lot of energy, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t have called it “euphoric” at the time, maybe just very optimistic. Almost brilliantly optimistic, really, like every obstacle was suddenly small and I could see the sensible way through everything.\n\nThough… I should say, if someone questioned me, I didn’t take it very well. I’d snap at them, as if they were trying to interfere or sabotage things. I’m not proud of that. At the time it felt like I was the only one seeing clearly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long do these periods of feeling optimistic and having a lot of energy typically last?\n\nPatient: It went on longer than I’d like to admit, really. After that investment loss, I think I was… not quite living like my usual retired self for about five months. Most of that time, everything seemed to revolve around trying to undo the damage.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of duration you’re asking about, doctor, but it wasn’t just a few days.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone would, especially when things weren’t going well. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear period of being sad in the way you mean.\n\nIs there something particular I should be looking for when you say depressed?\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel irritable during those times when you were lying awake before dawn and not feeling rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable is probably fair. I don’t like admitting that, because I thought of myself as focused, not angry. But if someone questioned what I was doing, I could snap at them pretty quickly.\n\nAt the time it felt like I was being sensible and optimistic, and everyone else was getting in the way. Looking back, I suppose I was acting as if I were the only reasonable person in the room. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that weren't really there during those periods of high energy or irritability?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was wound up and probably interpreting things too confidently, but I don’t recall hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there.\n\nIf there’s a more subtle version of that you mean, I can try to think about it, but nothing like voices or visions comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a persistent and profound sadness that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has been more than two weeks. I’ve tried not to call it “profound,” because that sounds rather dramatic, but since those plans fell apart, I’ve been low and… withdrawn, and feeling guilty.\n\nIt’s been going on about nine months now. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but I think that’s the accurate answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy during this period of feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but the things I used to genuinely enjoy just feel… flat now.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or get completely absorbed in photography and cooking. Even bridge, sometimes. Lately I’ll look at my camera or think about going to the bridge table, and I don’t feel pleasure so much as the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying it.\n\nI suppose that counts as losing interest, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer take pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it just that they feel like too much effort?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s just effort, because that sounds more… manageable, I suppose. But no, it’s more than that.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or lose myself in photography and cooking. Lately I look at my camera, or even think about the bridge table, and I feel almost nothing—except the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying myself.\n\nIs that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed. I could check properly if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I have.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but I keep going back over that investment decision in my head. Again and again. But that is where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost pleasure in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, really.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but the things that used to reliably absorb me—the museum, photography, cooking, even bridge—now I look at them and feel almost nothing. And that scared me enough that I told my wife I shouldn’t be left alone.\n\nIs that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your movement or activity level, such as feeling restless or slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yes… there has been some of that, I think.\n\nMy wife has noticed it more than I have. turn the arts council into a regional powerhouse, as though my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s mostly my sleep, doctor. It’s gotten… a bit out of hand, I think. At first I’d sleep two or three hours and then be up, making plans and feeling like I was ready to take on the day. I didn’t think it was such a terrible thing then.\n\nBut now it’s not feeling so useful. I’m lying awake before dawn with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. I thought I should come in before I let it go on too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing periods where you have a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I didn’t think of it as a problem at first, because it felt like I was being productive. But it was like I had this engine running inside me, if that makes sense, and sitting quietly felt almost impossible.\n\nI’d be up before sunrise, making calls, rewriting proposals, going from one thing to the next without really stopping. I know that may sound a bit excessive, but at the time it felt… purposeful. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those times when you had a lot of energy, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t have called it “euphoric” at the time, maybe just very optimistic. Almost brilliantly optimistic, really, like every obstacle was suddenly small and I could see the sensible way through everything.\n\nThough… I should say, if someone questioned me, I didn’t take it very well. I’d snap at them, as if they were trying to interfere or sabotage things. I’m not proud of that. At the time it felt like I was the only one seeing clearly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long do these periods of feeling optimistic and having a lot of energy typically last?\n\nPatient: It went on longer than I’d like to admit, really. After that investment loss, I think I was… not quite living like my usual retired self for about five months. Most of that time, everything seemed to revolve around trying to undo the damage.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of duration you’re asking about, doctor, but it wasn’t just a few days.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone would, especially when things weren’t going well. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear period of being sad in the way you mean.\n\nIs there something particular I should be looking for when you say depressed?\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel irritable during those times when you were lying awake before dawn and not feeling rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable is probably fair. I don’t like admitting that, because I thought of myself as focused, not angry. But if someone questioned what I was doing, I could snap at them pretty quickly.\n\nAt the time it felt like I was being sensible and optimistic, and everyone else was getting in the way. Looking back, I suppose I was acting as if I were the only reasonable person in the room. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that weren't really there during those periods of high energy or irritability?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was wound up and probably interpreting things too confidently, but I don’t recall hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there.\n\nIf there’s a more subtle version of that you mean, I can try to think about it, but nothing like voices or visions comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a persistent and profound sadness that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has been more than two weeks. I’ve tried not to call it “profound,” because that sounds rather dramatic, but since those plans fell apart, I’ve been low and… withdrawn, and feeling guilty.\n\nIt’s been going on about nine months now. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but I think that’s the accurate answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy during this period of feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but the things I used to genuinely enjoy just feel… flat now.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or get completely absorbed in photography and cooking. Even bridge, sometimes. Lately I’ll look at my camera or think about going to the bridge table, and I don’t feel pleasure so much as the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying it.\n\nI suppose that counts as losing interest, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer take pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it just that they feel like too much effort?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s just effort, because that sounds more… manageable, I suppose. But no, it’s more than that.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or lose myself in photography and cooking. Lately I look at my camera, or even think about the bridge table, and I feel almost nothing—except the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying myself.\n\nIs that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed. I could check properly if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I have.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but I keep going back over that investment decision in my head. Again and again. I tell myself I’ve ruined my wife’s security, that after all those years of trying to be the responsible one, I’ve made a fool of the whole thing.\n\nIt sounds rather harsh when I say it out loud, doesn’t it? But that is where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost pleasure in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, really.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but the things that used to reliably absorb me—the museum, photography, cooking, even bridge—now I look at them and feel almost nothing. Mostly I just think about how much effort it would take to act as if I were enjoying them.\n\nIs that what you mean by almost all activities?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I suppose I have, though I don’t like admitting that.\n\nIt’s not that I’ve made some grand plan, but there are mornings when I think it would be a relief if I simply didn’t wake up. And that scared me enough that I told my wife I shouldn’t be left alone.\n\nIs that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your movement or activity level, such as feeling restless or slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yes… there has been some of that, I think.\n\nMy wife has noticed it more than I have. She says I’ll pace through the house, sort of shuffling papers around as if I’m late for some board meeting, though of course I’m not. And then at other times I’ll sit at the kitchen table so still that she has to wait quite a while for me to answer even a simple question.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of change you mean, but yes, I suppose it’s been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were being controlled by someone or something else?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so.\n\nI mean, I’ve felt confused by my own thoughts at times, but not as if someone else was putting them there or controlling them. If that’s what you mean, then no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating or making decisions during this period of feeling low?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s been a major problem… at least, I haven’t noticed it clearly.\n\nI suppose some decisions feel harder than they should, but I can’t tell if that’s just ordinary worry or tiredness. Concentrating… maybe not as sharp as I used to be, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling low that lasted less than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Less than two weeks? No… I don’t think that’s really how it’s been.\n\nI wish I could say it was just a short spell, but the low feeling, and the guilt, and pulling away from things… that’s been going on for about nine months now, since those plans fell apart. I may be saying that too dramatically, but it hasn’t been brief.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling low that have significantly impacted your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I’d have to admit it has.\n\nI try not to make too much of it, but my wife has had to take over the finances, and she’s been staying close to me for safety. And I haven’t been showing up the way I used to—for the museum, the arts council, even with friends. I keep telling myself I’ll get back on track, but I suppose it has affected day-to-day life quite a bit.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s mostly my sleep, doctor. It’s gotten… a bit out of hand, I think. At first I’d sleep two or three hours and then be up, making plans and feeling like I was ready to take on the day. I didn’t think it was such a terrible thing then.\n\nBut now it’s not feeling so useful. I’m lying awake before dawn with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. I thought I should come in before I let it go on too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing periods where you have a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I didn’t think of it as a problem at first, because it felt like I was being productive. But it was like I had this engine running inside me, if that makes sense, and sitting quietly felt almost impossible.\n\nI’d be up before sunrise, making calls, rewriting proposals, going from one thing to the next without really stopping. I know that may sound a bit excessive, but at the time it felt… purposeful. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those times when you had a lot of energy, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t have called it “euphoric” at the time, maybe just very optimistic. Almost brilliantly optimistic, really, like every obstacle was suddenly small and I could see the sensible way through everything.\n\nThough… I should say, if someone questioned me, I didn’t take it very well. I’d snap at them, as if they were trying to interfere or sabotage things. I’m not proud of that. At the time it felt like I was the only one seeing clearly, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long do these periods of feeling optimistic and having a lot of energy typically last?\n\nPatient: It went on longer than I’d like to admit, really. After that investment loss, I think I was… not quite living like my usual retired self for about five months. Most of that time, everything seemed to revolve around trying to undo the damage.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of duration you’re asking about, doctor, but it wasn’t just a few days.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone would, especially when things weren’t going well. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear period of being sad in the way you mean.\n\nIs there something particular I should be looking for when you say depressed?\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel irritable during those times when you were lying awake before dawn and not feeling rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable is probably fair. I don’t like admitting that, because I thought of myself as focused, not angry. But if someone questioned what I was doing, I could snap at them pretty quickly.\n\nAt the time it felt like I was being sensible and optimistic, and everyone else was getting in the way. Looking back, I suppose I was acting as if I were the only reasonable person in the room. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that weren't really there during those periods of high energy or irritability?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was wound up and probably interpreting things too confidently, but I don’t recall hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there.\n\nIf there’s a more subtle version of that you mean, I can try to think about it, but nothing like voices or visions comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a persistent and profound sadness that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has been more than two weeks. I’ve tried not to call it “profound,” because that sounds rather dramatic, but since those plans fell apart, I’ve been low and… withdrawn, and feeling guilty.\n\nIt’s been going on about nine months now. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but I think that’s the accurate answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy during this period of feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but the things I used to genuinely enjoy just feel… flat now.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or get completely absorbed in photography and cooking. Even bridge, sometimes. Lately I’ll look at my camera or think about going to the bridge table, and I don’t feel pleasure so much as the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying it.\n\nI suppose that counts as losing interest, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer take pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it just that they feel like too much effort?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s just effort, because that sounds more… manageable, I suppose. But no, it’s more than that.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or lose myself in photography and cooking. Lately I look at my camera, or even think about the bridge table, and I feel almost nothing—except the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying myself.\n\nIs that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed. I could check properly if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I have.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but I keep going back over that investment decision in my head. Again and again. I tell myself I’ve ruined my wife’s security, that after all those years of trying to be the responsible one, I’ve made a fool of the whole thing.\n\nIt sounds rather harsh when I say it out loud, doesn’t it? But that is where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost pleasure in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, really.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but the things that used to reliably absorb me—the museum, photography, cooking, even bridge—now I look at them and feel almost nothing. Mostly I just think about how much effort it would take to act as if I were enjoying them.\n\nIs that what you mean by almost all activities?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I suppose I have, though I don’t like admitting that.\n\nIt’s not that I’ve made some grand plan, but there are mornings when I think it would be a relief if I simply didn’t wake up. And that scared me enough that I told my wife I shouldn’t be left alone.\n\nIs that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your movement or activity level, such as feeling restless or slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yes… there has been some of that, I think.\n\nMy wife has noticed it more than I have. She says I’ll pace through the house, sort of shuffling papers around as if I’m late for some board meeting, though of course I’m not. And then at other times I’ll sit at the kitchen table so still that she has to wait quite a while for me to answer even a simple question.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of change you mean, but yes, I suppose it’s been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were being controlled by someone or something else?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so.\n\nI mean, I’ve felt confused by my own thoughts at times, but not as if someone else was putting them there or controlling them. If that’s what you mean, then no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating or making decisions during this period of feeling low?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s been a major problem… at least, I haven’t noticed it clearly.\n\nI suppose some decisions feel harder than they should, but I can’t tell if that’s just ordinary worry or tiredness. Concentrating… maybe not as sharp as I used to be, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling low that lasted less than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Less than two weeks? Lately I’ll look at my camera or think about going to the bridge table, and I don’t feel pleasure so much as the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying it.\n\nI suppose that counts as losing interest, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer take pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or is it just that they feel like too much effort?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s just effort, because that sounds more… manageable, I suppose. But no, it’s more than that.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or lose myself in photography and cooking. Lately I look at my camera, or even think about the bridge table, and I feel almost nothing—except the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying myself.\n\nIs that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed. I could check properly if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I have.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but I keep going back over that investment decision in my head. Again and again. I tell myself I’ve ruined my wife’s security, that after all those years of trying to be the responsible one, I’ve made a fool of the whole thing.\n\nIt sounds rather harsh when I say it out loud, doesn’t it? But that is where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost pleasure in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, really.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but the things that used to reliably absorb me—the museum, photography, cooking, even bridge—now I look at them and feel almost nothing. Mostly I just think about how much effort it would take to act as if I were enjoying them.\n\nIs that what you mean by almost all activities?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I suppose I have, though I don’t like admitting that.\n\nIt’s not that I’ve made some grand plan, but there are mornings when I think it would be a relief if I simply didn’t wake up. And that scared me enough that I told my wife I shouldn’t be left alone.\n\nIs that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your movement or activity level, such as feeling restless or slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yes… there has been some of that, I think.\n\nMy wife has noticed it more than I have. She says I’ll pace through the house, sort of shuffling papers around as if I’m late for some board meeting, though of course I’m not. And then at other times I’ll sit at the kitchen table so still that she has to wait quite a while for me to answer even a simple question.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of change you mean, but yes, I suppose it’s been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were being controlled by someone or something else?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so.\n\nI mean, I’ve felt confused by my own thoughts at times, but not as if someone else was putting them there or controlling them. If that’s what you mean, then no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating or making decisions during this period of feeling low?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s been a major problem… at least, I haven’t noticed it clearly.\n\nI suppose some decisions feel harder than they should, but I can’t tell if that’s just ordinary worry or tiredness. Concentrating… maybe not as sharp as I used to be, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling low that lasted less than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Less than two weeks? No… I don’t think that’s really how it’s been.\n\nI wish I could say it was just a short spell, but the low feeling, and the guilt, and pulling away from things… that’s been going on for about nine months now, since those plans fell apart. I may be saying that too dramatically, but it hasn’t been brief.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling low that have significantly impacted your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I’d have to admit it has.\n\nI try not to make too much of it, but my wife has had to take over the finances, and she’s been staying close to me for safety. And I haven’t been showing up the way I used to—for the museum, the arts council, even with friends. I keep telling myself I’ll get back on track, but I suppose it has affected day-to-day life quite a bit.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current low feeling as mild, moderate, or severe?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I’m being honest, it feels severe.\n\nI don’t like saying that, because it sounds rather dramatic, but it doesn’t feel like just a mild sadness or something I can easily shake off. Severe is probably the most accurate word. Is that the sort of rating you mean?"
  }
